attending\nschool\nSW. 80\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nPART G: SMALL CONTRACTOR DEVELOPMENT\nG1001 SCOPE\nThis section covers construction aspects relating to the processes by which the construction industry\ndevelops emerging small contractors.\nIn terms of this contract, the Contractor shall effect such development by subcontracting portions of the\nWorks to Targeted Enterprises (as defined in clause G1002(a) below) such that the combined accumulated\nmonetary value of the work undertaken and achieved by such Targeted Enterprises equals or exceeds the\nmonetary value of the target set by the Employer for such work.\nG1002 DEFINITIONS AND APPLICABLE LEGISLATION\n(a) Definitions\nUnless inconsistent with the context, in these specifications the following terms, words or expressions shall\nhave the meanings hereby assigned to them:\nContract Participation\nContract Participation in terms of this contract is a process by which the Employer implements Government’s\nobjectives by setting a target relating to small contractor development which the Contractor shall achieve as\na minimum.\nContract Participation Goal (CPG)\nContract Participation Goal is the monetary value of the target set by the Employer in the Contract\nParticipation process.\nContract Participation Performance (CPP)\nContract Participation Performance is the measure of the Contractor’s progress in achieving the CPG.\nProject Management Team (PMT)\nA team established at the commencement of the contract, comprising a representative from each of the\nEmployer, the Employer’s Agent and the Contractor, that will be responsible for various functions related to\nthe implementation of the Contract Participation process.\nTargeted Enterprise\nA Targeted Enterprise is any company engaged by the Contractor as a subcontractor and which is registered\nwith the Construction Industry Development Board (CIDB) in a contractor grading designation equal to 1CE\nor 2CE or 3CE and which is also registered by the CIDB as Potentially Emerging (PE).\nThe CPG scope in terms of Part G of this contract has been reserved for execution using only the following\nprescribed numbers of Targeted Enterprises registered with the CIDB in each of the contractor grading\ndesignations indicated:\n• Grade 1CE PE (2 x Targeted Enterprise subcontractors prescribed) • Grade 2CE\nPE (2 x Targeted Enterprise subcontractors prescribed)\n• Grade 3CE PE (1 x Targeted Enterprise subcontractors prescribed)\nTargeted Labour\nSW. 81\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nTargeted Labour means individuals, employed by the Contractor and subcontractors in the performance of\nthe contract, who permanently reside in the target area or who are recognised as being residents of the\ntarget area on the basis of identification and association with and recognition by the residents of the target\narea, and excludes any labour employed by suppliers and manufacturers. Youth means persons between\nthe ages of 18 and 35.\n(b) Applicable Legislation\nThe following Acts, as amended from time to time, are predominant amongst those which apply to the\nconstruction industry and are listed here for reference purposes only:\n- The Constitution of South Africa;\n- Public Finance Management Act No. 1 of 1999;\n- Preferential Procurement Policy Framework Act No. 5 of 2000; - Construction Industry Development Board\nAct No. 38 of 2000; - Broad-Based Black Economic Empowerment Act No. 53 of 2003.\nG1003 CONTRACT PARTICIPATION\n(a) Objective\nIn order for the Employer to realise its social and development objectives, the Contractor is required to\nprovide business and labour opportunities through the participation of Targeted Enterprises and Labour in\nthe construction of the Works. The participation of Targeted Enterprises and Labour, measured in monetary\nterms, is specified by the Employer as a contract participation goal, for the Contractor to achieve.\n(b) Contract Participation Targets\nContract participation is the process by which the Employer Implements Government’s objectives. The\nEmployer sets a target for construction by specified entities, the rand value of which is based on the services\nand work undertaken by the specified entities. The target rand value will be measured as that specified\ntarget percentage of the Contractor’s final certified value of work completed (excluding CPA and VAT)\nmeasured at the date of issue of the Certificate of Completion. The Contractor is obliged to commit to or\nexceed the target stated in section C1.2.2 Contract Data, Part A: Data Provided by the Employer.\n(c) Contract Participation Goal (CPG)\nThe CPG is the monetary value of the target set by the Employer and will be calculated as follows:\nCPG = final contract value (excluding Contingencies, CPA and VAT) x (target % set by the Employer for\nTargeted Enterprises)\nThe final contract value is the total value of certified work measured at the date of issue of the Certificate of\nCompletion.\nThe value of the Provisional Sums scheduled under item G10.02 of Part G of the schedule of quantities shall\nnot necessarily make up the full value of the works required to meet the CPG target set by the Employer. It is\nthe Contractor’s responsibility to assess the work required to meet the CPG target and, if necessary, to\nengage Targeted Enterprises to execute work on the main contract as well to ensure that the CPG target is\nachieved.\n(d) Contract Participation Performance (CPP)\nThe CPP is the monetary value of the Contractor’s actual progress towards achievement of the CPG\ncalculated as follows:\nCPP = total value (excluding CPA and VAT) of contribution by Targeted Enterprises\nSW. 82\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nThe Contractor’s Contract Participation Performance will be measured monthly in order to monitor the extent\nto which he is striving to reach the CPG. The basis of monitoring shall be the levels of the individual\ncontributions for Targeted Enterprises. Monthly returns, in a format approved by the Employer, are required\nfrom the Contractor and shall be submitted with each interim payment certificate. Failure to adhere to this\nrequirement shall result in the delay of any payment due until the Employer’s Agent confirms that the\ninformation has been received.\nTo assist in the measurement of CPP, the Contractor shall include in his contract programme details of how\nhe will achieve the CPG. This shall include CPG achievement details for both the specific work indicated for\nCPG in terms of this contract for completion by Targeted Enterprises as well as details for any other work\nthat the Contractor may use towards achieving the CPG. The detail shall be provided not later than one\nmonth after the Employer’s Agent has accepted the original construction programme and shall be updated\nwith every subsequent revision of the programme.\nIn the event that the Contractor fails to substantiate that any failure to achieve the Contract Participation\nGoal (CPG) is due to quantitative under runs, the elimination of items contracted to Targeted Enterprises, or\nany other reason beyond the Contractor’s control which may be acceptable to the Employer, the Contractor\nshall be liable for a penalty as prescribed in clause SCC 4.1.1 of section C1.2.1.2 Special Conditions of\nContract. The penalty shall be calculated as follows:\nPenalty = 5% of the monetary value by which the achieved monetary value (CPP) falls short of the\ntarget monetary value (CPG)\n= 5% of (CPG – CPP)\nThe penalty shall be applied on a pro rata basis according to a monthly evaluation of achievements against\nthe programmed utilisation.\n(e) Accredited Registration\nCPP for Targeted Enterprises shall only be accepted if the respective Targeted Enterprises for which services\nor work is being claimed as having been performed are registered with the CIDB in one of the defined\ncategories. In addition, documentary evidence that such Targeted Enterprises are registered with the South\nAfrican Revenue Services (SARS) shall be lodged with the Employer’s Agent before the work or service may\nbe considered as having been performed by a Targeted Enterprise. The responsibility for producing\nevidence of such registration documentation shall rest with the Contractor.\n(f) Record keeping and Portfolio of Evidence\nThe Contractor shall assume responsibility for the compilation and maintenance of comprehensive records\ndetailing each Targeted Enterprise’s progress during construction, starting from the award of a subcontract to\na Targeted Enterprise until the successful completion of the subcontract work or termination of the\nsubcontract.\nThe Contractor shall keep comprehensive records of the training given to each trainee and, at the successful\ncompletion of each training course, each trainee shall be issued with a certificate indicating the course\ncontent as proof of attendance and completion. The Contractor shall keep a register of certificates issued.\nWhenever required, the Contractor shall provide copies of such records to the Employer’s Agent.\nThe Contractor is also required to develop and/or maintain a portfolio of evidence for each Targeted\nEnterprise as described in clause G1009.8 below.\nThe Contractor shall prepare and submit monthly to the Employer’s Agent in a form approved by the\nEmployer’s Agent, the following:\ni). a brief report which describes the commercially useful functions performed by the Targeted\nEnterprises and Labour in the performance of the contract, both during the interim period and on a\ncumulative basis;\nSW. 83\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nii). a schedule reflecting the estimated total value of the contracts, the cumulative value of the contracts,\nand the value of goods provided, and work and services performed over the period for which\npayment is claimed with respect to each and every Targeted Enterprise; and\niii). a schedule which lists the names, identity numbers, gender, trade/occupation, period of\nemployment, employment number and the like, as directed by the Employer’s Agent, together with\nthe respective wage rates payable in respect of Targeted Labour, including the monetary value of\nwages paid both on a cumulative basis and over the period for which payment is claimed.\nShould random inspections conducted by the Employer’s Agent on Targeted Enterprise and Labour activities\nindicate that such Targeted Enterprises and Labour are not performing in accordance with the requirements\nof the contract, the Contractor shall provide, in addition to the monthly reporting requirements, separate\nweekly resource returns and any other relevant information with respect to such Targeted Enterprises and\nLabour in a form approved by the Employer’s Agent.\nG1004 TENDER PROCESS FOR TARGETED ENTERPRISE SUBCONTRACTOR PROCUREMENT\nThe Contractor shall refer to the schedule of quantities contained in these Particular Specifications and to\nany other construction activities required to execute the Works in terms of this contract to determine how he\nintends to unbundle or package specific subcontracts for Targeted Enterprises and shall present his proposal\nto the PMT for approval.\nThe Contractor shall be responsible for compiling the tender documents that will enable him to engage the\nTargeted Enterprises.\nIn compiling the subcontract tender documents, the Contractor shall also include in each tender document\nany Tender Rules that may be relevant, and shall also include the proposed subcontract agreement. The\nContractor shall compile each subcontract tender document in such a manner that it shall facilitate the\nachievement of all objectives and principles pertaining to the development of the Targeted Enterprises.\nDraft tender documents shall be approved by the PMT before the Contractor invites tenders from Targeted\nEnterprises, who will be invited in consultation with the PMT and the local PLC.\nThe PMT shall conduct a compulsory briefing session to explain the Works required and the tender process\nto the Targeted Enterprises.\nTenders for the subcontracted works must close at a stipulated time and date. Tenders shall be placed in a\nsuitable formal Tender Box of design approved by the PMT, located at the Contractor’s site office. The\ntender opening shall be conducted by the PMT.\nThe Contractor shall evaluate the tenders and shall submit the tender adjudication report for each\nsubcontract to the PMT for review prior to award of each subcontract.\nIt shall be a condition of tender that the subcontractors appointed as Targeted Enterprises are registered with\nthe Construction Industry Development Board (CIDB) in a contractor grading designation equal to 1CE or\n2CE or 3CE or 4CE or 5CE or 6CE and are also registered by the CIDB as Potentially Emerging (PE).\nIt shall also be a condition of tender that Targeted Enterprises shall include in their tender submission the\nfollowing documentation:\n1. Valid letter of good standing from the Department of Labour.\n2. A tax compliance status report.\n3. A BEE verification certificate\n4. Bank account details certified by the bank.\n5. Certificate of registration of the tenderer in the required CIDB contractor grading designation,\nconfirming the tenderer’s registration Status as “Active” at the closing date of tender.\nSW. 84\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nThe tender documents shall also contain the proposed subcontract agreement, which shall also include for:\n(a) An entitlement of the subcontractor to receive such training as is contemplated in this contract. (b)\nAn obligation on the Targeted Enterprise to participate and co-operate in such training as is provided for in\nthis contract.\n(c) The allowable sources from which workers may be drawn in terms of the contract.\n(d) The terms and conditions relating to the recruitment, employment and remuneration of workers\nengaged on the contract.\n(e) The training to be provided to the temporary workforce.\n(f) The terms and conditions relating to payment of the Targeted Enterprise subcontractor.\nG1005 PROJECT MANAGEMENT TEAM (PMT)\n(a) Appointment\nA Project Management Team (PMT) is to be set up comprising a representative from each of the Employer,\nthe Employer’s Agent and the Contractor.\n(b) Duties and functions of the PMT\nThe duties and functions of the PMT are as follows:\n(i) determine the scope and extent of the works to be included in any particular subcontract;\n(ii) determine the target tender price according to the scope of work, and adjust the target rates\nwhere relevant;\n(iii) monitor the management of the tender process for the subcontracts to be executed by the\nTargeted Enterprises;\n(iv) adjudicate and approve tenders for Targeted Enterprise subcontracts; (v) monitor the\nmanagement of the subcontracts involving Targeted Enterprises; (vi) monitor the\ntraining, mentoring and development of Targeted Enterprises.\nG1006 general responsibilities of the contractor towards TARGETED ENTERPRISE\nSUBCONTRACTORs\n(a) Obligations\nThe Contractor shall ensure that he complies with the following obligations:\n(i) institute a quality assurance system;\n(ii) provide adequate training, coaching, guidance, mentoring and assistance to Targeted Enterprises;\n(iii) provide financial support and other assistance to ensure that the Targeted Enterprises are\nable to meet their obligations and commitments with respect to their subcontracts, including\nacquisition of labour, equipment and materials; and\n(iv) ensure that the contract participation goals and objectives are achieved.\nG1007 management of targeted enterprise subcontracts\nThe Contractor shall conclude the subcontract agreements, and provide the necessary management support\nto the Targeted Enterprise subcontractors. Failure by the subcontractor to comply with the terms and\nconditions of the subcontract agreement shall result in penalties being applied or the subcontract terminated.\nG1007.1 Compilation of Subcontract conclusion agreement\n(a) The Contractor in liaison with the PMT shall be responsible for the conclusion of each subcontract\nagreement. The agreement shall be in accordance with the provisions of subclause 4.4 of the\nGeneral Conditions of Contract for Construction Works 3rd Edition 2015 and shall be consistent with\nSW. 85\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nthe terms and conditions in this contract. The agreement shall be subject to approval by the Project\nManagement Team.\n(b) The terms and conditions of the subcontract agreement shall also specify the following:\n(i) an entitlement of the subcontractor to receive such training as is contemplated in this contract; (ii)\nan obligation on the Targeted Enterprise to participate and co-operate in such training as is provided for in\nthis contract;\n(iii) the allowable sources from which workers may be drawn in terms of the contract;\n(iv) the terms and conditions relating to the recruitment, employment and remuneration of workers\nengaged on the contract;\n(v) the training to be provided to the temporary workforce; and\n(vi) the terms and conditions relating to payment of the Targeted Enterprise subcontractor.\nG1007.2 Quality of work and performance of the subcontractor\n(a) The Contractor shall closely monitor and supervise all Targeted Enterprises and shall train, mentor,\nguide and assist each Targeted Enterprise in all aspects of management, execution and completion of his\nsubcontract. This shall typically include assistance with planning of the works, sourcing and ordering of\nmaterials, labour relations, monthly measurements and invoicing procedures. The extent and level of such\ntraining, mentoring, guidance and assistance to be provided by the Contractor shall be commensurate with\nthe basic level of subcontract applicable and shall be directed at enabling the Targeted Enterprise to achieve\nthe successful execution and completion of their subcontract.\n(b) The Contractor shall closely monitor and supervise all Targeted Enterprises and shall give\nreasonable warning to the Targeted Enterprise when any contravention of the terms and conditions of the\nsubcontract has occurred or appears likely to occur. The Contractor shall, whenever feasible, give the\nTargeted Enterprise reasonable opportunity to make good any such contravention, or to avoid such\ncontravention, and shall render all reasonable assistance to the Targeted Enterprise in this regard.\nG1007.3 Dispute avoidance and resolution procedures\n(a) When taking any disciplinary action or imposing a penalty as provided for in the subcontract, the\nContractor shall explain fully to the Targeted Enterprise that such actions are provided for in the subcontract.\nIf such action is contemplated this shall be discussed with the PMT before any action is taken.\n(b) If the subcontractor, in the opinion of the Employer’s Agent, fails to comply with any of the criteria\nlisted below, the Employer’s Agent shall issue a written warning to the Contractor, stating all the areas of\nnon-compliance. A copy of the letter of warning shall be forwarded to the Employer. The criteria are as\nfollows:\n(i) acceptable standard of work as set out in the specifications;\n(ii) progress in accordance with the time constraints in the subcontractor’s tender document;\n(iii) punctual and full payment of the workforce and suppliers;\n(iv) site safety; and\n(v) accommodation of traffic.\n(c) The subcontractor shall have 21 days from the date of receipt of the letter of warning by the\nContractor to address and rectify the issues raised by the Employer’s Agent, with the exception of the above\nsubclauses (b)(iv) and (v) for which the reaction time shall be in accordance with the relevant specifications\nfor those aspects of the Works but shall not be longer than 24 hours. Failure by the subcontractor to comply\nwith deadline set, will be sufficient grounds for the Contractor to apply a penalty, or terminate the subcontract\nprovided that the PMT is satisfied that the Contractor has made every effort to correct the performance of\nthe subcontractor.\n(d) The Targeted Enterprise shall have the right to dispute any ruling given or deemed to have been\ngiven by the Contractor, Employer’s Agent or Employer. Provided that, unless the Targeted Enterprise shall,\nSW. 86\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nwithin 21 days after his receipt of a ruling or after a ruling shall have been deemed to be have been given,\ngive written notice (hereinafter referred to as a Dispute Notice) to the Contractor, referring to this clause,\ndisputing the validity or correctness of the whole or a specified part of the ruling, he shall have no further\nright to dispute that ruling or the part thereof not disputed in the said notice.\nG1008 specific work to be carried out by targeted enterprise subcontractors\nFor this particular contract, the following listed work has been identified in terms of this Particular\nSpecification, Part G, as suitable for execution by Targeted Enterprises in order to assist the Contractor in\nachieving his CPG:\n1. Construction and clearing of drains.\n2. Installation of prefabricated culverts including inlet and outlet structures.\n3. Concrete channelling and concrete linings for open drains.\n4. Pitching, stonework and protection against erosion.\n5. Construction of gabions.\n6. Erection of road signs. 7. Road Marking\n7. Landscaping.\n8. Other work identified by the Employer to be executed in the community area.\nThe work to be carried out by Targeted Enterprises is not limited to the work listed above, and the Contractor\nmay need to engage Targeted Enterprises on other aspects of the main contract work in order to achieve his\nCPG.\nG1009 Training, coaching, guidance and mentoring\nG1009.1 Obligations\nThis section provides details of the Contractor's obligations of implementing the National Skills Development\nStrategy. This strategy sets priorities for the skills development of workers. The Sector Education and\nTraining Authorities (SETA) and provincial offices of the Department of Labour will play key roles in\ntransforming the vision of the strategy into reality.\nSupervision is included as part of the contractor’s general obligations for the administration and management\nof the Targeted Enterprises. However training, coaching, guidance and mentoring are not included as part of\nthe Contractor’s general obligations unless specific provision to that effect has been made in the project\nspecifications.\nThe Contractor shall, from the commencement of the contract, provide a structured development programme\ndesigned to improve the entrepreneurial and basic business management skills of identified Targeted\nEnterprises and hired labour that show initiative, as well as to improve their specific task skills (engineering\nskills) commensurate with the applicable levels of subcontract that will enable the Targeted Enterprises to\nachieve the successful execution and completion of their subcontracts. The ultimate objective of training,\ncoaching, guidance and mentoring is to develop the Targeted Enterprise subcontractors as far as their\npotential allows.\nG1009.2 Definitions\n(a) Training:\nTraining refers to the process of teaching a learner – usually in a classroom or simulated work environment\nsituation. Training usually takes place with one teacher/trainer and several learners. Principles and theory\nare taught. Demonstrations are given. Assignments are then set to ensure that the learner is able to apply\nwhat has been taught. Training is done by a specialist in the subject, who is also qualified to train.\nExample: Use of a dumpy level\nTraining would involve the theory of how a dumpy level works and how to calculate levels. A demonstration\nof how to set up and read a level could be given.\nSW. 87\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\n(b) Coaching:\nCoaching refers to hands-on training and is mostly on a one-on-one basis of tangible and measurable skills.\nIt is typically on-site training, or learning-on-the-job. Coaching is training by the process of “watch-do-\ncorrectpractice”. The coach does the task while the learner watches and asks questions. Then the learner\ndoes the same task while the coach watches. The learner is corrected until the coach feels that he has the\ngained competence in the function. The learner is then left to practise the task or skill, which the coach\noversees. Coaching is imparting a skill – usually manual or physical. A coach is usually a person doing the\nsame type of work on a higher or more competent level, or a person who has a mastery of the skill/task.\nWhile training gives the theory or shows/explains the principles, coaching helps the learner become\ncompetent and master a skill. A coach is often in the direct reporting line, i.e. someone in authority. Coaching\ncould be part of the management function and would make the job run more smoothly.\nExample: Use of a dumpy level\nCoaching would involve taking the learner onto site, setting up a dumpy level, reading levels. Then the\ncoach would get the learner to do the job, while the coach checked the setting up and reading. Once the\nlearner understands and can do the tasks, the coach checks periodically to ensure the learner is still doing\nthe job properly.\nThe desired outcome of coaching is for the learner to “fit-the-mould”, and to do things the same way and to\nthe same standard as the coach.\n(c) Guidance:\nGuidance is anticipating where one might go wrong, or where one is doing a task in a complicated, inefficient\nor ineffective way, and giving advice as to how to achieve a better result. Guidance is mostly given by a\nperson in the direct reporting line, but can be given by anyone. Guidance is not imparting skills, but\nsuggesting ways to improve performance.\nExample: Use of a dumpy level\nA person who knows how to use a dumpy level would be given guidance as to where he would next set up\nthe level to limit the number of set-ups. Guidance could be an extension of coaching.\n(d) Mentoring:\nMentoring is developing a person on a long-term career path. It is mostly about imparting skills that are often\nintangible and non-measurable. A mentor is seldom anyone in the direct reporting line – although he could\nbe a person several levels higher in that reporting line. Mentoring is more about developing a relationship\nand taking a personal interest in an individual. A mentor looks out for that individual and gives him advice\nthat cannot be found in a book or in the normal course of business. The sort of issues a mentor discusses\nwith the protégé include company politics, how to pick up work before others get wind of it, how to position\none’s company to get a better competitive advantage, how to tender such that the tender is lowest but the\nprofit is as good as or better than the next person, how to determine and assess risk. Mentoring helps the\nprotégé think strategically, sharpen entrepreneurial skills and grow – both personally and in terms of the\ncompany. A mentor helps the protégé to develop insight and shrewdness. It is more about business skills to\nsurvive long-term than the technical, tactical or day-to-day activities of doing business.\nThe desired outcome of mentoring is for the protégé to develop his own style, i.e., to shape his own “mould”.\n(e) Supervision:\nSupervision is instruction regarding the work to be performed, and ensuring it is carried out to specification\nand to the satisfaction of the supervisor. It is not instruction on how the work is to be performed, but rather\non what work has to be performed, and usually has specific time frames.\nG1009.3 Assessment of Targeted Enterprise potential\nSW. 88\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nIt is the responsibility of the Contractor to determine the level of development required by each Targeted\nEnterprise. This will require that the Contractor determine the level at which the Targeted Enterprise can\noperate competently and determine the skills already acquired by the Targeted Enterprise subcontractor in\nterms of training and on-the-job experience. In some circumstances Adult Basic Education Training (ABET)\nwill need to be started before any formal training can commence. The assessment of each appointed\nTargeted Enterprise is to be produced by the Contractor for discussion at the first Project Management Team\nmeeting following the appointment of the Targeted Enterprise.\nG1009.4 Development Plan\nWithin a month of the first PMT meeting on the Targeted Enterprise assessments, the Contractor is to\npresent a development plan, specific to each Targeted Enterprise. The development plan is to be reviewed\nby the PMT for appropriateness before being implemented.\nThe development plan is to include the following:\n specify the development needs of each Targeted Enterprise contracting entity – the systems the\nentity lacks;\n specify the development needs of the individuals comprising the entity – the skills the individuals\nwithin the entity lack;\n the level to which that activity will be developed within the period of the contract; ▪ whether training,\ncoaching, guidance and/or mentoring is to be given in each activity; ▪ the person/s responsible\nfor each activity.\nG1009.5 Identification and general training of potential Targeted Enterprises\n(a) The progression of training, coaching and mentorship may need to start with the identification and\ngeneral training of potential Targeted Enterprises and hired labour that show initiative, and should\nend with their acquisition of sufficient management skills that will equip them to compete confidently\nfor subcontract work beyond the duration of this contract. In addition, generic skills shall be taught\nwhere the need for these has been identified as being necessary amongst Targeted Enterprise\nworkforces and hired labour.\n(b) Before commencing with any structured training the Contractor shall submit his intended programme\nto the Project Management Team for approval of its subject content and proposed trainers, and the\nContractor shall, if so instructed by the PMT, alter or amend the programme and/or course content. The\nContractor shall be responsible for the provision of everything necessary for the delivery of the various\ntraining workshops and modules including:\n(i) Provision of a suitable fully serviced training venue facility as specified in this Part G;\n(ii) Procurement of suitable accredited trainers;\n(iii) Provision, in conjunction with the accredited trainers, of all the necessary training materials,\nincluding stationery and study materials; and\n(iv) transportation of the learners to and from the training venue facility (it is therefore in the Contractor’s\ninterest that the training venue facility be provided on the site in order to reduce these transportation\ncosts).\nPayment to the Contractor for carrying out the training function shall be effected through the pay items\nprovided in this Part G.\nG1009.7 Activities\nThe tasks for each of the activities making up the development plan are described below in further detail.\nG1009.7.1 Technical\nSW. 89\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nG1009.7.1(a) Technical Administrative Functions\nGeneral Tasks\n(i) Understanding and interpreting drawings:\nOn Roadwork Construction Contracts, the reading of drawings is required at high levels of Targeted\nEnterprise development. The relevant drawings that would need to be understood include all relevant\nroadwork and concrete brick wall details for construction. Understanding and interpreting drawings includes\nthe following:\n Understanding the cross referencing to drawings from the contract documents and the bill of\nquantities;\n Understanding the layout of the drawings;\n Understanding the plan view and elevations and cross sections; ▪ How to interpret, accurately lay out\nand construct the details.\n(ii) Setting out construction works:\nThis skill requires an understanding of the function and use of instruments such as the\ndumpy level, the theodolite, and procedures such as stringing a line.\n(iii) Technical know-how of all relevant Roadwork construction activities, and typically includes:\n Traffic control/safety precautions;\n Environmental management;\n Clearing and grubbing;\n Excavations and backfilling;\n Pre-cast and in-situ cast concrete works (including reinforcing where applicable);\n Storm water channelling and catch pits / chutes;\n Mass earthworks and pavement layers;\n Asphalt paving;\n Concrete block paving;\n Concrete brick laying;\n Plastering and bag washing;\n Guardrails;\n Fencing;\n Finishing up and tidying of works.\nThe development plans shall maximise the exposure of each Targeted Enterprise to as many activities as is\npracticable.\n(iv) Task organization:\nThis activity involves the correct supervision of labour and organisation of tasks to ensure that labour is\nutilised most effectively. This includes the following:\n• Allocation of the appropriate number of people for the task;\n▪ Availability on site of the correct type and amount of material and equipment at the point of use;\n▪ Planning tasks ahead so that labour does not stand around waiting.\n(v) Task prioritization:\nTask prioritization must cover an understanding of critical paths, including identifying which tasks can be\npostponed and which are urgent. It includes planning and monitoring so that time targets are achieved. It\nmust also cover gaining an understanding of the penalties applicable to late completion of tasks.\n(vi) Work schedule:\nThis is the ability to produce daily work plans and will assist with task organization. Daily work plans need to\nbe developed into weekly and monthly work plans and vice versa. These plans assist in material ordering,\nplant scheduling, labour allocation, timeous completion of the work, and keeping track of profits/losses.\n(vii) Effectiveness:\nSW. 90\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nEffectiveness is ensuring that a job gets done right, or properly. Effectiveness\nis doing the right things.\n(viii) Efficiency:\nEfficiency is ensuring that the job is done with the least effort and cost possible. Efficiency\nis doing things right.\n(ix) Quality control:\nQuality control requires knowledge of specifications and tolerances and ensuring that the work adheres to\nthese, as well as understanding the requirements of the Employer.\n(x) Measuring work done for payment certificates:\nThis is the ability to measure actual work done and to translate these measurements into a payment\ncertificate. Knowledge must be gained of how to calculate escalation, how to claim for VOs and how to\nhandle other claims.\n(xi) Site meetings:\nKnowledge needs to be gained of the following:\n The importance of site meetings;\n Identifying the relevant personnel to attend site meetings;\n Preparing for a site meeting;\n Understanding progress reports including why they are important and how to prepare them;\n Recording issues discussed at the meeting;\n Implementing and follow through of issues recorded;\n Understanding the process of the meeting and when to bring up various concerns is needed.\n(xii) Handling site instructions and VOs:\nThis is the ability to distinguish between site instructions and variation orders and how to respond to such\ninstructions.\nMaterials Tasks\n(xiii) Materials planning:\nThis includes the following:\n▪ Determining the quantity of materials required for each task and planning ordering; ▪\nDetermine appropriate lead times to ensure that everything required to do a job is on\nhand; ▪ Quantity take-offs for pricing a bill at higher levels of development.\n(xiv) Receiving, storing and handling materials:\nReceiving includes checking materials delivered against the delivery note and the order placed. Storage\ninvolves knowing what quantities and type of materials to store, planning accessibility, and safety of\nmaterials from theft, the weather, etc. Handling of materials needs to be carried out to ensure no wastage or\ndamage.\n(xv) Waste control:\nThis includes the importance of waste control to reduce costs and the management of reducing waste of\nmaterials in storage and in use.\n(xvi) The handling/installation of precast units:\nKnowledge of precast units, handling and installation must be gained.\nEquipment Tasks\n(xvii) Machine Analysis:\nSW. 91\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nThis is the analysis of the use of equipment. It includes calculating fuel and hourly costs. This will enhance\nan understanding of the effective use of equipment, how to reduce costs and provide a background for\npricing of equipment for tenders.\n(xviii) Maintenance Schedule:\nThis covers the importance of maintenance of equipment and how to schedule this so that there is minimal\ndisruption in day-to-day work.\n(xix) Appropriate and correct use of tools and equipment: Provide coaching on the correct use of tools\nand equipment.\n(xx) Care of tools and equipment:\nCare of tools and equipment includes the proper handling, cleaning, storage, stacking, etc.\nThe coaches of the above listed activities are generally the Foreman, Site Clerk, Mechanic, Construction\nManager, Plant Manager or Contracts Manager.\nG1009.7.1(b) Technical Management Functions\nGeneral Tasks\n(i) Site set-up:\nThis involves gaining an understanding of site set-up procedures and principles, including establishing a site\noffice and facilities, providing access to the site, initial layout of the works, storage facilities, security, etc.\n(ii) Construction program / work plan:\nKnowledge of how to develop a construction program / work plan, monitor and assess the production, and\ncorrect the programme where necessary.\n(iii) Understanding tests:\nUnderstanding what tests are required, reading and interpreting results relating to specifications and quality,\nand how to make the corrections required.\n(iv) Productivity:\nRecording productivity and understanding the principles. Knowing where to make corrections and how to\nimplement solutions.\nMaterial Tasks\n(v) Materials schedule:\nThis involves gaining knowledge of how to develop a materials schedule from a Bill of Quantities, the\nDrawings and any other relevant contractual document. It also includes linking the works program to an\nordering schedule, and sourcing of suitable materials.\nEquipment\n(vi) Determine appropriate levels of equipment:\nThe determination of the appropriate type and number of tools, and equipment required.\n(vii) Proper use of equipment:\nThis is gaining knowledge, understanding and competency in the proper use of equipment.\n(viii) Productivity of equipment:\nThe productivity of equipment must be understood to know how this affects his ability to perform.\n(ix) Allocation of equipment:\nThis involves the ability to competently allocate equipment to the various tasks.\nSW. 92\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\n(x) Waste control:\nWaste control is an essential aspect of running a site well and making a profit. The principles of this,\ntogether with the practical ways to control waste need to be understood.\nThe coach/mentor of these activities is generally the Construction Manager, Contracts Manager, or\nEquipment Supplier.\nG1009.7.2 Financial\nG1009.7.2(a) Financial Administrative Functions\n(i) Basic Finance:\nThis includes knowledge of the definition and difference between debtors and creditors as well as gaining\ncompetency in basic bookkeeping. Knowledge of keeping and managing accounts and other financial\ndocuments also needs to be developed. A basic financial course may be appropriate to develop in this area.\n(ii) Bank account:\nThe opening of a bank account, making deposits, the difference between stop-orders and debit-orders,\noverdraft facilities and generally managing a bank account needs to be understood. This can also be done\nthrough a course, and/or with a supportive bank manager, and/or with the appointed coach/mentor.\n(iii) Orders and systems:\nThis will require the setting up of systems and procedures that will take into consideration delivery leadtimes,\nnon-availability of items, alternate sources, preparation of storage areas ready for delivery, and the\narea/volume of storage space.\n(iv) Invoices:\nThe development of systems within the Targeted Enterprise entity that will check materials received versus\nwhat was ordered, check quantities delivered, and check actual prices paid against quotes/tender prices.\n(v) Stock Control:\nThe importance of controlling stock needs to be understood. Systems to implement stock control need to be\nimparted.\n(vi) Payroll:\nThis involves the establishment of a payroll system within the Targeted Enterprise entity. This could include\ndeveloping an internal system, outsourcing the payroll or learning a commercially acquired payroll system.\nThe system needs to record days worked, pay rate, legal deductions and a leave register.\n(vii) Legal requirements:\nThe financial requirements of legal obligations of companies must be imparted to the Targeted Enterprise\nsubcontractor. This includes payment of company tax, VAT (Value Added Tax), Skills Development Levy\n(SDL), Work Unemployment Insurance Fund (UIF), Workman’s’ Compensation (WC), and any applicable\nmunicipal levies as well as the applicable minimum wages for the area (also refer below to G1009.7.5\nLegislative).\n(viii) Payment certificates:\nThis includes the compilation and submission of a payment certificate. As well as knowing how to determine\nwork done to date, how to present it in terms of the Bill of Quantities, and how to claim for materials on site.\nG1009.7.2(b) Financial Management Functions\n(i) Claims and VOs:\nSW. 93\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nThe following types of claims may be applicable: Claims for additional work done, inclement weather, etc.,\nclaims for Variation Orders implemented. Understanding claims includes knowing when a claim can be\nsubmitted, the procedure and paperwork for the submission of a claim and which claims are worth pursuing.\n(ii) Cash Flow:\nThe concept of cash flow, as well as the development of cash flow projections and the monitoring of the cash\nflow are vital aspects of running a business. Included under this topic is knowing how long it takes from\nplacing an order for material until payment is due, and time lags between ordering materials and using the\nmaterials and receiving payment for work done. The concept of sufficient working capital also needs to be\nimparted.\n(iii) Costing System:\nCosting systems must be set up and understood. Aspects include capturing costs, monitoring profit,\nanalysing costs and using this information to determine productivity levels.\n(iv) Tendering:\nA knowledge is required of how to build up rates, how to obtain prices, how to negotiate better discounts,\nhow to be creative to achieve the desired result more cost effectively, how to calculate and accommodate\nP&Gs, and what Provisional Sums are and how they are handled.\n(v) Budgeting:\nThe difference between tendering and budgeting must be understood as well as gaining skills on how to\ndevelop a budget. Knowledge of how to monitor actual expenses against budgeted expenses, how to\nanalyse the differences, and the value budgeting is needed.\n(vi) Risk assessment:\nThis involves learning how to assess the financial risks associated with the job in terms of his performance\nand profitability and the management of these risks. This becomes increasingly important as the Targeted\nEnterprise grows.\n(vii) Insurance:\nThis includes an understanding of why insurance is required, how to obtain insurance, the benefits and\ncosts, insurance providers and what can be claimed from different types of insurance. The different types of\ninsurance to be included are Short term insurance, Public Liability and Contractors All Risk insurance.\n(viii) Interest Rates:\nInterest Rates can have a dramatic impact on the profit margins. Therefore an understanding must be\ngained of what interest rates are, how they impact on profit margins, how to cater for fluctuating interest\nrates in preparing a tender, and how to find and/or negotiate the best interest rates.\n(ix) Bridging Finance:\nThis includes understanding what bridging finance is and the different forms of bridging finance. Guidance\nshould be given on when bridging finance should be used, where it can be obtained, what securities are\nneeded, and how to go about securing funds.\n(x) Sureties:\nThis involves understanding what Sureties are, why and when they are needed, how they can be obtained,\nwhat they cost and how they can be redeemed.\n(xi) Procurement of Plant and Equipment:\nThe difference between purchasing, leasing, hire purchase and hiring plant needs to be understood. Also\nincluded is gaining knowledge of all aspects of these options as well as the benefits and implications for the\nbusiness for each option.\n(xii) Procurement:\nSW. 94\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nProcurement of materials includes sourcing suppliers, getting quotes, placing an order and negotiating\ndiscounts. The managing contractor can assist in introducing higher level Targeted Enterprises to the\nrelevant materials suppliers.\n(xiii) Subcontract:\nAt the lower levels, this will involve the Targeted Enterprise understanding the subcontract agreement\nbetween himself and the managing contractor and the obligations of this document. With a Targeted\nEnterprise operating at a higher level, this will include sourcing other subcontractors, as well as interpreting\nthe tender/quote, adjudication and awarding of subcontracts.\n(xiv) Productivity:\nThis includes taking production rates and translating them into financial terms to help build up rates for\ntendering and to monitor profit margins.\nThe coach and/or mentor for these functions are typically the Bookkeeper, Site Clerk, Foreman, Construction\nManager, Contracts Manager, Accountant, Estimator and Buyer.\nG1009.7.3 Human Resources\nG1009.7.3.(a) Human Resource Administrative Functions (i)\nLabour Supervision:\nThis involves the supervising of labour effectively. This includes knowing the level of competencies of labour,\nbeing able to motivate workers to ensure that productivity levels are achieved and maintained. It also\nincludes the allocation of tasks to labour.\n(ii) Team Sizes:\nThis is ensuring the size of the work team is appropriate for the matching activity and understanding that\nteams that are too large are unproductive and teams that are too small are not effective.\n(iii) Productivity:\nThe principles of productivity and how it is best achieved for various tasks is to be understood and applied. It\nalso involves being able to determine realistic productivity levels, such as square metres of grass cut with a\nmachine per hour, length of guardrail installed per day, etc.\n(iv) Time sheet:\nA system of time sheets which accurately records the time each employee spends on the job needs to be in\nplace.\n(v) Leave Registers:\nA system for recording leave needs to be in place, which includes the dates that leave was taken, what type\nof leave it was and whether the employee qualifies for paid or unpaid leave in terms of legislation.\n(vi) Employee records:\nThis involves knowing and complying with legislation for conditions of employment. Employment contracts\nmust be in place for all labour employed and a formal system of keeping employee records must be\nestablished. Employee records must be in line with legislation including recording of employee details and\ncopies of identification documents, termination of service procedures, certificates of service etc.\n(vii) Disciplinary procedure:\nKnowledge of the legal aspects of Disciplinary procedures is essential. The ability to set up these\nprocedures, and ensure that all staff understand and adhere to them is required. Warning systems must\ninclude records of verbal warnings, warning forms, disciplinary hearings, dispute resolutions and termination\nprocedures.\n(viii) Training:\nThis involves understanding the concept of the Skills Development Levy, and how to claim from CETA for\ntraining provided. Service Providers have CETA funded Skills Facilitators available to assist Targeted\nEnterprises in identifying training needs and compiling work place skills plans (WPSP) at no cost to the\nSW. 95\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nTargeted Enterprise. An understanding of the importance of providing training for staff with regards to AIDS\nawareness, Safety, Productivity, Development and Accountability is essential.\n(ix) Community Liaison:\nThe management and importance of Community Liaison must be understood.\nG1009.7.3(b) Human Resource Management Functions\n(i) Labour recruitment:\nEmployment of labour with the appropriate skills is essential. Therefore sourcing of appropriate labour and\nknowledge of how to assess the range of skills and level of competency in potential employees needs to be\ngained.\n(ii) Labour allocation:\nAllocation of labour is a skill that needs continual refining. It requires knowing the specific strengths of staff\nemployed, the numbers of labourers and level of skill required for specific tasks, as well as being able to\nbuild effective cohesive teams that understand their roles without continual supervision.\n(iii) Labour schedules / planning:\nThis involves learning how to compile labour schedules from the works program and the consequent\nallocation of staff to the work planned.\n(iv) Histogram / manpower plan:\nA manpower plan includes establishing how many people are needed on the job at what times. This is an\nimportant skill even at the most elementary level. As the Targeted Enterprise develops, the management of\nmoving people from site to site in such a way that there are neither too many nor too few labourers and/or\nsupervision becomes an important skill to obtain.\n(v) Management of supervision:\nThis activity involves understanding the principles of supervision, and how to effectively manage to ensure\nthat these principles are applied and/or developed by the appointed supervisor.\n(vi) Productivity:\nProductivity applies to labour as well as tasks and plant. Skills must be developed on how to determine\nappropriate levels of labour productivity and how to ensure that staff achieves these levels.\n(vii) Labour Law:\nA basic understanding of labour legislation, specifically Labour Relations Act (LRA), and Basic Conditions of\nEmployment Act (BCEA), Employment Contracts, and Termination of Service is essential as well as an\nunderstanding of the implications of these laws on business.\n(viii) Accountability systems:\nThis requires that accountability systems are developed and implemented for supervisory staff.\nCoaching, guidance and mentoring on the above listed functions are typically undertaken by the Foreman,\nConstruction Manager, Site Clerk, HR Manager, Trainer and Contracts Manager.\nG1009.7.4 Contractual\nG1009.7.4(a) Contractual Administrative Functions\n(i) Relationships and communication:\nThis involves the development and maintenance of good relationships and the establishment of good\ncommunication systems within the Targeted Enterprise business. This includes communication between the\nTargeted Enterprise and the other role players such as the Managing contractor, the Employer’s Agent and\nthe Employer. An understanding of the importance of good communication is also important. Good\nSW. 96\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\ncommunication starts with the right attitude, and involves listening as well as talking. It is also listening to\nwhat is not being said. Allowances need to be made for differences in culture and economic backgrounds.\n(ii) Setting up contractual administration requirement (systems):\nContractual administration systems need to be established and implemented. This includes the use and\nimportance of site dairies and keeping rainfall/weather records, as well as developing progress reports.\n(iii) Implementing EMP:\nThis activity involves understanding the design and implementation of an Environmental Management Plan.\nG1009.7.4(b) Contractual Management Functions\n(i) Contract Law:\nKnowing and understanding the content and implications (including costs) of the Conditions of Contract and\nthe Specifications that will be required. This will include understanding the content and implications of\npenalty clauses.\n(ii) Risk Assessment:\nThis activity involves being able to assess the contractual risks associated with the contract both in terms of\nperformance and in terms of the risks associated with the Employer.\n(iii) Managing Contract Administration:\nManaging Contract Administration ensures that all the required reports, such as accident reports and\nprogress reports are completed and submitted.\n(iv) Administration of subcontractors:\nIdentification and separation of the responsibilities of the managing contractor from the responsibilities of the\nsubcontractors needs to be understood and applied. The management of subcontractors to ensure that\ncontractual obligations are met is essential knowledge to be gained by higher level Targeted Enterprises.\n(v) Subcontractor agreements:\nUnderstanding the contents of the subcontract agreement between the Targeted Enterprise and the\ncontractor is an important skill to be learnt.\n(vi) Disputes:\nThis activity involves understanding the procedures used in handling disputes or disagreements with the\nContractor and/or the Employer.\nThe Contracts Manager and Construction Manager would normally be involved in this function.\nG1009.7.5 Legislative\nG1009.7.5(a) Legislative Administrative Functions\n(i) Legal Registrations:\nThe legal requirements for registration of companies must be imparted to the Targeted Enterprise\nsubcontractor. This includes company tax, VAT (Value Added Tax), PAYE, Skills Development Levy (SDL),\nUnemployment Insurance Fund (UIF), Workman’s’ Compensation (WC), and any applicable municipal levies.\nAn understanding of what each registration is, what benefits he gets from being registered, and how to\nregister must be developed.\nG1009.7.5(b) Legislative Management Functions\n(i) Labour Law:\nKnowledge of the labour legislation and the implications for the business is required. This includes Skills\nDevelopment Levy (SDL), Work Place Skills Plans (WPSP), Unemployment Insurance Fund UIF),\nSW. 97\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nWorkman’s’ Compensation (WC), Labour Relations Act (LRA), Basic Conditions of Employment Act (BCEA),\nand the applicable minimum wages for the area.\n(ii) OHS Act:\nAll aspects of the OHS Act (dealing with safety and health) and the implications must be understood. A\nsafety officer who has the appropriate level of understanding and knowledge must be appointed within each\nTargeted Enterprise.\n(iii) Environmental Management Plan:\nAn environmental management plan needs to be submitted regarding how the environment will be managed\nfor the duration of the contract, and what restoration will take place at the end of the contract. An\nunderstanding of the importance and content of this plan must be gained.\n(iv) Adjudication/Arbitration:\nThis activity involves knowing what legal recourse is available when disputes/disagreements are not\nresolved. This knowledge must include the processes to be followed and where to go to implement\nproceedings.\nThese functions will generally require the input of the company lawyer, company accountant, HR manager,\nHealth and Safety Specialist, and Environmental Specialist.\nG1009.7.6 General\nG1009.7.6(a) Administrative Functions\n(i) Filing systems:\nThis activity includes establishing company filing systems that are efficient and effective. It also includes\nknowing the importance of filing, what must be filed and how it should be filed.\n(ii) Office set-out:\nKnowledge of the layout of the site office buildings position and the organisation of the offices and systems\nwithin the buildings is to be imparted.\n(iii) Record keeping:\nThis activity involves developing knowledge of what records need to be kept and how best to keep them.\n(vi) Good housekeeping:\nGood housekeeping is about developing a work approach of keeping the site tidy, cleaning putting away\ntools and working in an orderly way. Developing good housekeeping assists a company in working efficiently\nand improving profit margins.\n(v) Communications with staff:\nThe importance of good communication with staff needs to be understood as well as the effects of good\nrelations with the staff in terms of loyalty and productivity. An understanding must also be gained that good\ncommunication enhances supervision and management of the work.\n(vi) Communications on site:\nThis activity involves establishing communication systems between the site and the office and understanding\nthat effective communication on site leads to good relations and contributes to the smooth running of the\nsite.\nThe Construction Manager, site clerk and HR Manager will train/coach or mentor the above listed functions.\nG1009.8 Portfolio of Evidence\nThe Contractor is to develop and/or maintain a portfolio of evidence for each Targeted Enterprise. The\nPortfolio of Evidence is a collection of proof of the training, coaching, guidance and mentoring inputs\nSW. 98\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nprovided to the Targeted Enterprise and is similar to a log book used by technical students completing\nexperiential training. It is a living document which records the development progress of the Targeted\nEnterprise and will need to be updated continually throughout the duration of the contract. It remains the\nproperty of the Targeted Enterprise and they take it with them to their next contract.\nThe Portfolio of Evidence should include the following documentation:\n• The development path designed for each Targeted Enterprise;\n• The training courses completed by the Targeted Enterprise;\n• The hours of guiding, coaching and mentoring received for each activity listed in the\ndevelopment plan;\n• A list of outcomes achieved at each level for each activity;\n• Six-monthly progress reports on the development provided (inputs) versus the actual progress\nmade (output) by the Targeted Enterprise;\n• A list of competencies.\nG1009.9 Training Requirements\nOnly qualified trainers employed by training agencies that are accredited by the relevant Sector Education\nand Training Authorities (SETA), or other institutions recognised by the Department of Labour shall deliver\nany training. “Accredited training” refers to both the trainers as well as to the training material. Certificates\naffirming the successful participation in the various courses shall be presented to each attendant.\nThe Contractor shall facilitate in the delivery of training by instructing and motivating the hired labour and\nrelevant Targeted Enterprises regarding attendance and participation. All training shall take place within\nnormal working hours, or as agreed with the trainees.\nG1009.10 Development training\nThe Contractor will arrange training to support the development of the Targeted Enterprise. This training\nmust be provided by training providers accredited with the relevant SETA pertaining to the course material\nbeing presented. The training will take place at hours agreed with the trainees, possibly on a part time basis\nafter hours.\nThe following training courses are recommended to support the development programme detailed in the\ncontract document:\n• NQF Level 2: Construction Contractor - Business owner and administration officer;\n• Tender training NQF Level 3 – Business owner / Technical expert;\n• Computer literacy training - Business owner and admin officer – Microsoft Windows, email,\nMicrosoft Word and Microsoft Excel;\n• General bookkeeping relevant to construction - Business owner and admin officer;\n• Tendering NQF Level 4 and 5 – Business owner / Technical expert;\n• Construction supervision (Roadworks) NQF Level 4 – Business owner / Technical expert.\nG1009.11 Safety Training\nThe Targeted Enterprise safety representative is to be fully trained in all aspects of safety and his duties in\nthis regard. The owner of the Targeted Enterprise is to be trained on their responsibilities regarding safety\nregulations.\nG1009.12 Engineering skills training\nThe focus of training provided on the contract should be to support the development of the Targeted\nEnterprise. Engineering skills training will only be approved by the PMT when appropriate.\nThe Targeted Enterprise, their workforce and hired labour that show initiative will be entitled to receive\nstructured training that will improve on-task skills necessary for the execution and successful completion of\nthe works. The Contractor, in conjunction with the Employer’s Agent, shall monitor the progress of the hired\nSW. 99\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nlabour and each Targeted Enterprise closely and shall identify those who, in their collective opinion, will\nbenefit from structured engineering skills training as may be provided for in the contract, and where required\nby the Employer’s Agent shall make recommendations in this regard. The final list of candidates shall be\ndecided between the Contractor and the Employer’s Agent, and those selected shall receive formal\nengineering skills training in a programmed and progressive manner throughout the duration of the contract.\nThe training programme shall offer complete courses that could comprise some or all of the following\nmodules:\n(i) Use and maintenance of hand tools;\n(ii) Operation of equipment;\n(iii) Manufacture and installation of minor precast concrete units;\n(iv) Erect, dismantle and maintain formwork;\n(v) Basic concrete skills;\n(vi) Excavation, backfill and compaction;\n(vii) Bricklaying;\n(viii) Erosion protection using stone pitching, gabions or renos.\nG1009.13 Training venue facility\nThe training venue facility to be provided by the Contractor shall be constructed, furnished and fully serviced\nfor the duration of the contract in accordance with SABS 1200 standard specifications. The facility shall\naccommodate a class of up to 25 learners and shall comprise the following:\n(a) Lecture room (interior area) = 48 m2\n(b) Ablutions (male) = 6 m2\n(c) Ablutions (female) = 6 m2\n(d) Chairs for learners (individual chairs, with backs) = 25 off\n(e) Desk area for 25 learners (500 mm width) = 12,5 m2\n(f) Chairs for trainers and management (individual chairs, with backs) = 5 off\n(g) Table area for trainers and management = 3 m2\n(h) 220/250 volt power points = 6 off\n(i) Double 80 watt fluorescent light fittings complete with ballast and tubes = 6 off\n(j) Single incandescent light fittings complete with 100 watt globes = 4 off\n(k) Wash hand basins complete with taps and drains = 4 off\n(l) Fire extinguishers, 9,0 kg, all purpose dry powder type, complete, mounted on wall with brackets =\n2 off\n(m) Air conditioning units with 2,2 kW minimum capacity, mounted and with own power connection =\n4 off\n(n) Voltage stabilizers = 2 off\n(o) Floodlights complete with poles and 500 Watt minimum globes and controlled by photocells =\n2 off\n(p) White boards (3 m x 1,5 m) = 1 off\n(q) Venetian blinds = 12 m2\nG1010 measurement and payment\nItem Unit\nG10.01 Procurement of Targeted Enterprise subcontractors as described in Part G:\n(a) Contractor’s charge for the management and execution of the Targeted Enterprise procurement\nprocess:\n(i) Procurement process for the totality of all tenders concluded for the appointment of CIDB contractor\ngrading designation 1CE Targeted Enterprise subcontractors (2 x individual tenders prescribed, 150 copies\nof the tender document required for each individual tender) Prov. Sum\nSW. 100\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\n(ii) Procurement process for the totality of all tenders concluded for the appointment of CIDB contractor\ngrading designation 2CE Targeted Enterprise subcontractors (2 x individual tenders prescribed, 150 copies\nof the tender document required for each individual tender) Prov. Sum\n(iii) Procurement process for the totality of all tenders concluded for the appointment of CIDB contractor\ngrading designation 3CE Targeted Enterprise subcontractors (1 x individual tenders prescribed, 150 copies\nof the tender document required for each individual tender) Prov. Sum\nThe unit of measurement shall be the number of individual subcontract agreements concluded with Targeted\nEnterprise subcontractors in accordance with the procurement process described in this Part G.\nEach tendered rate shall be in full compensation for the management and execution of the Targeted\nEnterprise procurement process in the relevant CIDB contractor grading designation scheduled, including for\nthe compilation, printing, binding and issue of the tender documents for each tender, for the advertising of\neach tender, for the provision of the venue and the conducting of each compulsory briefing session for\ntenderers, for the conducting of each tender opening process, for the adjudication of the tenders received for\neach tender, for the preparation of each tender adjudication report and the review thereof in conjunction with\nthe PMT, for the award of each tender and for the conclusion of the subcontract agreement with each\nsuccessful Targeted Enterprise tenderer.\nItem Unit\nG10.02 Construction Works for Targeted Enterprises:\n(a) Payments associated with the construction Works carried out by Targeted Enterprise subcontractors\nappointed in terms of Part G PC sum (PC Sum)\n(b) Handling costs and profit in respect of subitem G10.02(a) above percentage (%)\n(c) Supply of materials and small plant to assist Targeted Enterprise subcontractors appointed in terms\nof Part G provisional sum (Prov. Sum)\n(d) Handling costs and profit in respect of subitem G10.02(c) above percentage (%)\n(e) Contractor’s charge for the management of the Targeted Enterprise subcontractors appointed in\nterms of Part G lump sum (Sum)\nExpenditure under subitems G10.02(a) and (c) shall be in accordance with clause 6.6 of the General\nConditions of Contract, 3rd Edition 2015.\nThe provisional sum for subitem G10.02(a) is provided to cover the total cost of the construction Works\ncarried out by the Targeted Enterprises as certified by the Employer’s Agent, in separate payments for each\nTargeted Enterprise in accordance with Part G of the Particular Specifications.\nThe tendered percentage for subitem G10.02(b) is the percentage of the amount actually spent under\nsubitem G10.02(a), and shall include full compensation for the handling costs of the Contractor and the profit\nin connection with the construction Works carried out by the Targeted Enterprise subcontractors.\nThe provisional sum for subitem G10.02(c) is provided to cover the total cost of the materials and small plant\nsupplied by the Contractor to assist the Targeted Enterprises as certified by the Employer’s Agent, in\nseparate payments for each Targeted Enterprise in accordance with Part G of the Particular Specifications.\nThe tendered percentage for subitem G10.02(d) is the percentage of the amount actually spent under\nsubitem G10.02(c), and shall include full compensation for the handling costs of the Contractor and the profit\nSW. 101\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nin connection with the supply of materials and small plant by the Contractor to assist the Targeted Enterprise\nsubcontractors.\nThe tendered lump sum for subitem G10.02(e) shall include full compensation for the registration of all the\nsubcontract agreements and the management of all the Targeted Enterprise subcontracts, including for the\nprovision of the necessary management support, coaching, guidance and mentoring to the Targeted\nEnterprise subcontractors.\nItem Unit\nG10.03 Training of learners employed by Targeted Enterprise subcontractors:\n(a) Generic skills:\n(i) Training costs prime cost (PC) sum\n(ii) Handling costs and profit in respect of subsubitem G10.03(a)(i) above percentage (%)\n(b) Entrepreneurial skills:\n(i) Training costs prime cost (PC) sum\n(ii) Handling costs and profit in respect of subsubitem G10.03(b)(i) above percentage (%)\n(c) Engineering skills:\n(i) Training costs prime cost (PC) sum\n(ii) Handling costs and profit in respect of subsubitem G10.03(c)(i) above percentage (%)\n(d) Training venue facility, including the cost of transporting the learners to and from this facility\nlump sum (Sum)\n(e) Transportation and accommodation costs of selected learners only, while receiving off-site\ntraining:\n(i) Transportation and accommodation costs provisional sum (Prov. Sum)\n(ii) Handling costs and profit in respect of subsubitem G10.03(e)(i) above percentage (%)\nExpenditure under subsubitems G10.03(a)(i), (b)(i), (c)(i) and (e)(i) shall be in accordance with clause 6.6 of\nthe General Conditions of Contract, 3rd Edition 2015.\nThe prime cost sum for each of subsubitems G10.03(a)(i), (b)(i) and (c)(i) is provided to cover the total costs\nof the required training in generic skills, entrepreneurial skills and engineering skills respectively, including\nfor the procurement of the services of the accredited trainers and their delivery of the training courses to the\nlearners employed by Targeted Enterprise subcontractors, the provision of all training materials including all\nstationery and study materials, and the wages of the learners for the duration of the courses. The payment\nof wages to learners in terms of subsubitems G10.03(a)(i), (b)(i) and (c)(i) will only be made to those\nlearners who attend and successfully complete each course of the approved training programme. Payment\nshall not be made to learners who, once selected, do not attend or only partially complete structured training\ncourses.\nThe Contractor’s own personnel shall be permitted to attend the training courses provided for the learners\nemployed by the Targeted Enterprise subcontractors only to the extent that the venue and its facilities can\nefficiently and comfortably accommodate the additional number of learners. All additional costs relating to\nsuch attendance by the Contractor’s own personnel shall be carried by the Contractor and shall not be\nSW. 102\n\nCONTRACT NO.: GKM 05-26/27\nPART C3: SCOPE OF WORK\nTHE AVENUE ROAD REHABILITATION WARD - 3\nconsidered for payment in terms of item G10.03, including for any additional costs related to the accredited\ntrainers and their delivery of the training courses, the provision of additional training materials, and the\nwages of the learners from the Contractor’s own personnel for the duration of the courses.\nThe tendered percentage for each of subsubitems G10.03(a)(ii), (b)(ii) and (c)(ii) is the percentage of the\namount actually spent under each of subsubitems G10.03(a)(i), (b)(i) and (c)(i) respectively, and shall\ninclude full compensation for the handling costs of the Contractor and the profit in connection with the\nprovision of the training in generic skills, entrepreneurial skills and engineering skills respectively, including\nfor the costs of record keeping and reporting with respect to the training received by each learner, and the\ncosts of the compilation of the portfolio of evidence with respect to each Targeted Enterprise.\nThe tendered lump sum for subitem G10.03(d) shall include full compensation for the provision of the training\nvenue facility complete and serviced as specified, including for the provision of power, water, sewerage and\ncleaning services for the duration of the contract, for lighting, power points and voltage stabilizers, for air\nconditioning, blinds, fire extinguishers, floodlights, furniture and whiteboards, for the provision of security at\nthe facility, for all other costs necessary to maintain the facility for the duration of the contract, and for the\nremoval of the facility on completion of the contract. The tendered lump sum shall also include full\ncompensation for transporting the learners on each day of training from their place of work to this training\nvenue facility, and back again after the training for the day has been delivered by the accredited trainers.\nPayment of the lump sum shall be made in three instalments as follows:\nThe first instalment, 50% of the lump sum, shall be paid after the contractor has met all his obligations\nregarding the provision of the training venue facility, complete and serviced as specified, and the facility has\nbeen successfully commissioned for use.\nThe second instalment, 35% of the lump sum, shall be paid when 75% of the training courses proposed in\naccordance with the Contractor’s structured training programme, as approved by the PMT, have been\ndelivered to the learners by the accredited trainers.\nThe third and final instalment, 15% of the lump sum, shall be paid when training for all the Targeted\nEnterprise subcontracts has been concluded and the facility has been dismantled and removed from the site.\nThe provisional sum for subsubitem G10.03(e)(i) is provided to cover all costs related to the transportation\nand accommodation costs of selected learners only, while receiving off-site training, where such learners\nhave been specifically selected in conjunction with the PMT to receive such off-site training and where such\ntraining cannot be delivered using the training venue facility provided by the Contractor in terms of subitem\nG10.03(d).\nThe tendered percentage for subsubitem G10.03(e)(ii) is the percentage of the amount actually spent under\nsubsubitem G10.03(e)(i), and shall include full compensation for the handling costs of the Contractor and the\nprofit in connection with the transportation and accommodation costs of selected learners only, while\nreceiving off-site training.\nAdd the following new payment item:\nITEM UNIT\nG10.04 Penalties:\n(a) Fixed penalty for
